# Engine re-homing: start's tests onto the engine part each asserts

The user approved splitting start's own tests ("yes split start tests") and, on 2026-10-30, chose this design over keeping only the test split. Two thirds of the e2e test phases sit under `cmd-start`, because each one launches a full run, and the start command's runner links its whole closure. This document is part of the target design, like [the target layout](modularization-target-layout.md) and [the test split](modularization-test-split.md). It moves each start, reply and adopt test that asserts one engine part onto that part's crate, as a crate-level test over a recorded fixture. Every other test stays as a smaller, named set of full journeys through the real binary. Like the rest of the design this is design-only: no file under `src/`, `crates/` or `tests/` changes, and no test is removed, sampled or sharded.

## The rule

**What a test asserts.** `scripts/modularization/engine-tests.mjs` reads each test body of the `cmd-start`, `cmd-reply`, `cmd-adopt` and `cross-command` suites at the reviewed commit. It takes the string literals inside the test's `assert!`, `assert_eq!` and `assert_ne!` invocations and in the `let` bindings those invocations name. Each literal is matched to the target-layout crates whose production code names it: a crate's files and the items it takes from other files, less comments, `#[cfg(test)]` modules and the items other crates take from its files. A literal named by more than two crates (`"status"`, `"state"`) is a common key, not evidence, and is ignored. The engine part a test asserts is the set of crates its remaining literals name.

**Where it goes.** The test moves to the engine crate with the smallest closure that contains every crate it asserts. It becomes a crate-level test of that crate (`<crate>/tests/<module>.rs::<test>`) over a recorded fixture (`tests/recorded/engine/<crate>/<module>/<test>/`). As in the test split, an engine crate whose tests hold more than 3% of a full Linux run splits into one suite per source module (`engine--scheduler--lifecycle`).

**What stays.** A test stays a full journey through the binary, in its existing test-split part, for one of four reasons: it asserts live ordering, which the catalog already rules no fixture may replace; it asserts a literal only a command or the dispatcher names; its assertions name no literal an engine crate names; or no single engine crate's closure contains everything it asserts. Every `cross-command` test carries a live-ordering reason, so none moves; those suites keep the integrations they prove. A test whose read cannot be attributed with certainty keeps the wider input: the full journey.

**One place per test.** `engine-tests.mjs --check` refuses an `engine-tests.json` the rule would not derive. It also refuses one that drops or repeats a test, files a test under the wrong parent, moves a live-ordering test, or names a crate that is not an engine crate of the layout. Every one of the 2,104 test phases of those four suites is either moved or kept, and the tables below list both sets.

## How a moved test keeps its assertions

**Its code changes, and its assertions do not.** A moved test keeps its name and every assertion, and only its setup changes. It no longer starts the binary through the harness's `World`. Instead it loads the recorded fixture (the run root, journal lines, reply envelopes or plan documents its journey produced up to the asserted point) and calls the engine crate's own entry point. It then compares the result against the same literals. "Every test that leaves the binary" below lists each moved test with the crate-level test that keeps its assertions and the fixture it reads. Moving it is the code change this document records. The test-split document's tables already cover the tests that change only their suite.

**Where the fixtures come from.** A recorder mode of each original journey drives the real binary through its existing setup and writes the fixture instead of asserting. It runs in the moved test's engine suite and fails when the fixture it writes differs from the committed one. The engine crate's strict decoders read the fixture, the same decoders that refuse unknown fields in production. So a format change in a producing crate fails the moved test at once, and the format's golden test fails with it.

**Where the fixtures are re-verified against the real binary.** On every pull request that runs the moved test's engine suite. That covers any change to the engine crate or its closure, which holds every crate the test asserts, and any change to the fixture, the test's code, a harness item it calls or a test fake its journey starts. The recorder target declares the engine suite's inputs, not the binary's whole closure; that narrowing is what the saving comes from. Each recorder costs about what the original test cost, and the replay charges exactly that, so the generated skip shares are the measured cost of this per-PR re-verification. The crate-level tests' own seconds are unmeasured and add to it. The release PR's full sweep re-records every fixture as well. What surfaces only there is a change on a re-homed scenario's path outside its engine crate's closure (a command, the dispatcher, or an engine crate it does not reach) that alters what the binary records. The generated "Where the recorded fixtures are re-verified" section states this per test and names the engine crates that keep no full journey.

## What it measures, candidly

Re-homing raises estimated Linux seconds skipped from the test split's 11.32% to the figure in the first generated table. That is small, and the replay says why; the generated "Why a typical PR still selects nearly every second" subsection names what the typical PR changed and the crates nearly every suite reaches. The engine crates that most PRs change (publication, run-control, edit-reconciler, run-projection, and the plan and event crates) sit in the closures of most engine suites as well as of the kept full journeys. The engine suites hold under a third of a full run's seconds (the generated engine-suite table gives the total), and the kept journeys link start's whole closure, so the median PR still selects nearly every second. The generated "Crates whose change still selects more than half the suites" table names each such crate, with how many PRs changed it, the further split that narrows its reach, and the part that stays a real shared input after that split together with who reads it; the replay credits none of those splits with a saving. Re-homing more tests needs assertions that can be attributed: apart from the live cross-command journeys and those no single engine crate covers, the kept tests either assert a command's own output or name no literal an engine crate names. The 30-minute line is still not reached by selection. As the test-split document says, that needs per-test and per-job cost reduction (the parallel `onepipeline-test-cost` work) or a coverage trade-off only the user can make.

## Migration

Steps 8 to 10 follow the test split's seven steps, one per parent, and each can land on its own. A step moves one parent's attributed tests into their engine crates' `tests/` directories, adds those tests' recorded fixtures and the recorder mode, and adds the engine suites to Nx with the recorders in them. Each step keeps the binary's behaviour, because no production code changes; the moved tests call entry points the engine crates already expose after the crate layout's steps. It keeps the published crate's public API, because the facade still re-exports every item. It keeps every test, because `engine-tests.mjs --check` holds each test phase to exactly one place and the recorder keeps every original scenario running through the binary. Each step's skip share is in the generated migration table.

## Reproduce

Run from a pinned clone (`node scripts/modularization/pinned.mjs -- ...`), because the evidence describes the reviewed source:

- `node scripts/modularization/engine-tests.mjs --write` derives `engine-tests.json`; `--check` refuses a stale or invalid one.
- `node scripts/modularization/engine-replay.mjs STEP.json --parents cmd-start[,cmd-reply[,cmd-adopt]]` replays the 144 PRs with those parents' tests re-homed.
- `node scripts/modularization/engine-report.mjs --collect START.json REPLY.json ADOPT.json` writes `engine-evidence.json` and this document; `--check` refuses a document that differs from what the evidence renders.

### Crates whose change still selects more than half the suites

**Projected**: what a change to the crate alone selects, and how many of the 144 PRs changed its source, its resolved dependencies or its manifest requirement, with the further split that narrows its reach and what stays a real shared input after that split. Every production crate here is in the closure of cmd-start, cmd-reply, cmd-adopt, whose kept full journeys (cmd-start 727, cmd-reply 144, cmd-adopt 114 tests) link the whole command, so they read every part of it after any split; every test-fake crate here is one those journeys start or link. The replay credits none of these further splits with a saving: the figures below are for the layout as designed, without them. Only moving more of those journeys onto engine crates, which needs attributable assertions (see the kept reasons below), frees them.

## Where the recorded fixtures are re-verified

Each of the 768 re-homed test phases has a recorder: its original journey, driven through the real binary, which writes the fixture and fails when it differs from the committed one. The recorder runs in its engine suite, so a pull request runs it whenever it runs the crate-level test. That is any change to the engine crate or a crate in its closure, which holds every crate the test asserts (`engine-tests.mjs --check` refuses one outside it). It also runs on a change to the fixture, the test's own code, a harness item it calls or a test fake its journey starts. The replay charges each selected engine suite each re-homed phase's full measured end-to-end seconds, which is what its recorder costs. The skip shares above are therefore the measured cost of re-verifying the fixtures on those pull requests. The crate-level tests' own seconds are not measured and add to it.

Only a change outside all of those inputs that still alters what the binary records for a re-homed scenario is left for the release PR's full sweep, which re-records every fixture. Such a change is to production code on the journey's path outside the engine crate's closure: a command, the dispatcher, or an engine crate the engine crate does not reach. These engine crates keep no full journey asserting them, so their re-homed tests' recorders are their only end-to-end check:

| Engine crate | Re-homed test phases with no kept journey asserting the crate |
| --- | --- |
| dispatch-cancellation | driver::an_attached_launch_returns_once_its_observer_graph_has_stopped_and_the_run_advances, lifecycle::a_node_that_states_its_own_body_publishes_it_and_spends_no_dispatch, lifecycle::a_title_the_sibling_will_not_commit_under_is_refused_before_any_dispatch, shipped::a_rule_testing_a_label_that_is_not_selectable_is_refused_by_name |
| watch-lease | unwatched::a_reused_pid_another_host_an_empty_token_another_run_a_schema_and_a_stamp_are_not_live__phase_cmd_start, watch::a_run_with_no_dispatch_or_settlement_reports_no_last_progress__phase_cmd_start, watch::a_watch_refuses_a_malformed_or_non_loopback_ordering_peer__phase_cmd_start |
